[trialAccount t18230910-249]
1218. MARGARET KELLY was indicted for stealing, on the 27th of August, a purse, value 6 d.; a sovereign, a crown, a shilling, and two sixpences, the property of James Smith, from his person.
JAMES SMITH. I live in High-street, St. Giles's, and am a hair-dresser. On the 27th of August, about half past eleven o'clock at night, I was going home down Oxford-street - between Charles-street and High-street I was accosted by the prisoner - she asked me to go home with her - I desired her to leave me; she insisted upon walking with me; I stopped when I got to Bainbridge-street; I stopped there that she might not know where I lived, being just at home. I desired her to leave me - she went off in a very abrupt manner, which rose my suspicion. I put my hand to my right hand trowsers pocket, and my purse, which contained a sovereign, a crown, and two shillings, was gone; it must have been taken while I stood endeavouring to get rid of her - I did not feel her hand in pocket - I stopped with her nowhere else. I followed her immediately - she was not above four or five paces off - she ran into a private house, the door of which stood open; I followed her up four or five stairs - did not like to go further, but returned to the door and called the watchman - two came in, and found her concealed at the top of the garret stairs; she said she supposed I only wanted my money, and it was down stairs; she had concealed it under the stairs at the bottom; we went down, and she shewed the watchman where it was; my money was still in it. I swear that I had no intention to have any thing to do with her.
EDWARD YARRANTON. I am a watchman. I heard a cry of Watch! and found the prosecutor standing at the door, of No. 6, Bainbridge-street, which is not a bad house, but a lodging-house for labouring people. I went up and found the prisoner at the top of the stairs; he said that was the person who robbed him of his purse. She said he only wanted his property, and she would give it him. I found it under the stairs.
JOSEPH LOCKETT. I am a watchman. I saw the purse found.
JAMES SMITH. It is my purse, and contains the money.
Prisoner's Defence I asked him for some drink, and parted with him; and at the corner of the street, I stopped down, and picked up the purse.
JAMES SMITH. It could not have fallen out of my pocket.
GUILTY. Aged 23.
Transported for Seven Years.

1220. RICHARD MADDISON was indicted for stealing, on the 1st of July, two pair of boots, value 8 s., the goods of John Powell.
JOHN POWELL. I live in Play House-yard, Golden-lane, and am a boot-maker; the prisoner lodged with me; I worked in his room. On the 1st of July, about six o'clock in the evening, I placed these boots in a box by my side, and missed them in the morning; he slept there next night, and appeared an industrious man.
SIMON PARSONS. I lodge in the same room. The prisoner came home in the evening, went out and returned between ten and eleven o'clock, and left in the morning before the boots were missed - we all worked in the room.
WILLIAM SHARP. I am a pawnbroker. On the 1st of July, between seven and eight o'clock in the evening, the prisoner pawned these boots for 4 s.; he said he had them to make for a lady, who was not at home; and he wished to pawn them till morning.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I found the duplicate of them among the tools, but did not pawn them.
GUILTY. Aged 27.
Confined One Month.

HENRY BONHAM. I am a shoemaker. and live in Dean-street, Soho. On the 12th of August, about six o'clock in the evening, the witness brought the prisoner in with these boots, which had hung on an iron rail under the door, and could be reached from outside.
HANNAH CHANTRY. I live in Dean-street, opposite Bouham's. I saw the prisoner make four attempts to getthe shoes, and the fifth succeeded; she stood loitering about with them under her arm. I took hold of her.
(Property produced and sworn to.)

1222. HENRY WOOD was indicted for stealing, on the 26th of July, fourteen umbrella covers, value 16 s.; twenty-one rings, value 1 s.; a flat iron, value 1 s., and an apron, value 6 d., the goods of James White.
JANE WHITE. I am the wife of James White, who lives in Coleman-street, we lodge on the second floor. On the 24th of July, I went out about half past ten o'clock in the morning, returned in an hour - I had left the key with a lodger, for my husband; I found the door open and this property gone. I make umbrellas, the bolt of the lock was shot.
JAMES WHITE. I am a carpenter, I was out till twelve o'clock, when these things were missed. I apprehended the prisoner on the 26th of July, in Type-street; he said, he would take me to where he sold the property, and took me to Needham's - he was a stranger.
THOMAS NEEDHAM. I am an umbrella maker, and live in Rosemary-lane. On the 24th of July, between four and five o'clock, the prisoner brought me fourteen umbrella covers, and twenty-one rings, and asked 10 s. for them; I offered 5 s. - he came back and took the money, and brought White to me afterwards. He said, he took them for a debt of a man who was dead.
MARY SHEW. I lodge with White, and had the key - I gave it to Maunders.
MARY MAUNDERS. Shew gave me the key - I did not part with it till White came home.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I know nothing of the theft - I had them from a person who said a man was dead. I said I was doing it for another person.
GUILTY. Aged 45.
Confined Six Months and Whipped.

1223. JOHN WHITE was indicted for stealing, on the 6th of August, six table cloths, value 20 s., and three towels. value 1 s., the goods of Thomas Harrison, the elder, and Thomas Harrison, the younger.
THOMAS HARRISON, JUN. I am in partnership with my father Thomas; we keep the Tavistock-hotel, Covent-garden . The prisoner was in our service, as laundryman with his wife, and left about six months ago - we missed a large quantity of linen while he was with us; and in consequence of information, went to his lodgings in August; he was not at home, but his name was on the door, and his wife and daughter there - he keeps a chandlers-shop. On the first floor of the house I found six table cloths, and three towels, some marked Tavistock-hotel, at full length, and others with other initials.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. When did you miss them - A. I cannot say, as we missed a quantity at different times.
JOHN PURTON. I am an officer. On the 6th of August, I went to the prisoner's house with a search warrant - I found a towel in the back parlour, a phosphorous box, and a bunch of keys, and the other things on the first floor - there were no lodgers in the house. I waited till he came in, and told him he was suspected of stealing these things; he was much agitated, and wished to put away a pocket book which he had. He afterwards said, he was in great trouble, and sorry for what he had done.
RICHARD LIMBRICK. I went with Purton. The prisoner said he was in great distress, and his wife knew nothing about it. I found the duplicate of a table cloth on him.
HENRY IVES. I am servant to Mr. Beecham, pawnbroker, Holborn. On the 29th of June, a table cloth was pawned in the name of Collins. I do not know who by.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
GUILTY. Aged 38.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18230910-255]
1224. RICHARD ALDERTON was indicted for stealing, on the 9th of August, a saw, value 5 s. 6 d., and two squares, value 3 s., the goods of Joseph Dore.
JOSEPH DORE. I am a journeyman carpenter. I lost my tools from a building in Kingsland-road on Saturday, the 8th of August, where I was at work. They were on the first floor at twelve o'clock when we went to dinner, and at one o'clock they were gone. The prisoner had no business there. I found my saw an hour after.
BENJAMIN MATSON. I am a carpenter. I was working at this building, and left about twelve o'clock. I lost a saw. The prisoner had formerly worked on the premises. I have seen him lurking about since.
WILLIAM BLENKHORN. I am a carpenter. I was at work about fifty yards from this building, and found the prisoner lying asleep among the shavings, about five o'clock in the morning, and turned him out.
JAMES ESSEX. I am an officer. I took the prisoner on Sunday morning, in Bethnal-green, and found the duplicate of a saw upon him.
RICHARD CARPENTER. I am shopman to Mr. Thimbleby. On the 9th of August, between twelve and one o'clock, the prisoner pawned the saw for 2 s.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
GUILTY. Aged 19.
Confined One Year and Twice Publicly Whipped.
[trialAccount t18230910-256]
1225. THOMAS BELFIELD was indicted for stealing on the 24th of June, a pocket-book, value 6 d.; a purse value 1 s., and two sovereigns and five shillings, the property of William Cansdale, from his person.
WILLIAM CANSDALE. I live on Swan-terrace, Great Dover-road, and am collecting-clerk at a brewery. On Tuesday, the 24th of June, between nine and ten o'clock in the evening. I was sober. I was at the fire in Red Lion-street, Holborn. There was a great crowd. I got into the midst of it, and was endeavouring to make my way out. My pocket-book was in my left inside coat pocket, and my purse containing two sovereigns and five shillings in my left side breeches pocket. My coat was buttoned, and my breeches pocket also. They were safe when I entered the crowd. There was a general rush among the crowd; and immediately as I got out of the crowd, I found both my pockets had been robbed. I gave information at the different offices, and found the prisoner in custody on the Saturday following, at Marlborough-street, and mypocket-book produced, containing letters and memorandums directed to me.
FRANCIS JOHN GATES. I am patrol of St. Giles's. On the night of this fire, between ten and eleven o'clock, I was on duty in George-street with Riley, and the prisoner crossed the street, coming down Stacey-street, in a direction from the fire. I stopped him, and asked what he had under his coat. He said he had nothing there. I put my hand to his side, and found this pocket-book full of papers. He said it was his own, and contained a few letters from his friends. I asked what they were about. He said he did not choose to tell. I found nothing else upon him. The direction on the letters led me to the prosecutor.
PHILIP RILEY. I was with Gates. His statement is correct. When I took him to the watch-house, he persisted that it was his own property, and after questioning him, I asked where he found it. He then said he found it at the end of Plumtree-street, between Charlotte and Streatham-streets.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I picked it up.
GUILTY. Aged 20.
Transported for Life.
[trialAccount t18230910-257]
1226. JOHN CLARKE was indicted for stealing, on the 12th of July, a pair of boots, value 12 s.; a pair of shoes, value 7 s., and a shilling and a sixpence, the property of John Hawkins.
JOHN HAWKINS. I am a shoe-maker, and live in Bull's-head-court, Great Peter-street. The prisoner lived two doors off, and worked with me. I had not seen these boots for a month, but missed the shoes on the 4th. The money was marked, and was taken from the till. On Sunday, the 12th, he was taken up, and I charged him with taking 1 s. 6 d. from the till. He immediately threw it back on the shelf, and said it was only 1 s. 6 d. I had been looking through a hole, and saw him leave his seat, and heard the till unlocked. He was the only person in the room. His wife gave me a duplicate.
LEONARD MEEDES. I am shopman to Messrs. Graham and Co., pawnbrokers. On the 11th of June the prisoner pawned the boots, and on the 21st the shoe were pawned by somebody in his name.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
GUILTY. Aged 21.
Confined Eight Months.

1227. ELIZABETH DAVIS was indicted for stealing. on the 9th of August, two table-cloths, value 6 s.; a knife. value 3 d., and a fork, value 3 d., the goods of William Acton.
WILLIAM ACTON. I live in Maiden-lane, Covent-garden, and keep a cook-shop. The prisoner was my charwoman; we missed things at different times. I went to her lodgings with a warrant, and found a knife and fork, which I knew, and two table-cloths in pawn, which I know to be mine. I claimed them in her presence, and she admitted them to be mine.
JOHN GRINDLEY. I am shopman to Messrs. Townsend and Co. pawnbrokers, Russell-street. On the 4th of July the prisoner pawned a table-cloth, and another on the 9th.
ROBERT DUKE. I am an officer. I took the prisoner into custody, and found the duplicate of the table-cloth on her; she said she only took one - and when the second was produced, she said she only took two; she appeared distressed.
GUILTY. Aged 41.

1228. WILLIAM DENNISON was indicted for stealing, on the 24th of June, four books, value 3 s., and a waistcoat, value 6 d., the goods of Matthew Weldhen.
MATTHEW WELDHEN. I lived in Red Lion-street, Holborn. The prisoner is no acquaintance of mine - but I know his features. A fire broke out in the house a little after eight o'clock - I was out at the time, and only know that these things were on the second floor at the time. On the Saturday following I found the prisoner in custody at Marlborough-street with them.
JOHN KENDRICK. I am a beadle of St. Giles's. A watchman brought the prisoner to the watch-house in Smart's Buildings, on the night of the fire, about eleven o'clock, with a bundle containing a waistcoat and four books; I asked what he had there - he said it was his own; before it was untied, I asked what was in it - he then said somebody gave it him in Holborn. I took him to Marlborough-street. The prosecutor claimed them, I found other things in the bundle, which were lost at the fire; he could not tell what was in it; he had a coat on his back which he said was given him at the same time - but he could not tell who by.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I had them given to me. I was taken a quarter of a mile from the fire.
GUILTY. Aged 31.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18230910-260]
1229. JOHN FUZDEN was indicted for stealing, on the 11th of August, four half crowns, four shillings, and seven sixpences, the monies of John Ingram, from his person.
JOHN INGRAM. I live in Lambeth-street, Whitechapel. On the 28th of August, about two o'clock in the morning, I came home quite sober - my money was in my breeches pocket - I left my door ajar - I got up at eight o'clock in the morning, and the money was gone.
MARY INGRAM. I am the wife of the prosecutor. I got up a little before eight o'clock, and his money was gone. The prisoner had been in the house helping my son, and was there the day before. He usually came to call my son up.
ANN GOLDING. I lodge on the second floor - I came down about six o'clock in the morning, and saw the prisoner come out of Ingram's room - I knew him very well, but did not speak to him.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18230910-261]
1230. JOHN HURLEY was indicted for stealing, on the 13th of August, a handkerchief, value 2 s., the goods of Thomas William Bevill, from his person.
THOMAS WILLIAM BEVILL. I live at Bayswater, and am not in business. On the 13th of August I was crossing George-street, St. Giles's - I felt my coat skirt moving - turned round, and saw the prisoner attempting to throw my handkerchief into a door-way - it fell on his heels - I picked it up and secured him; he resisted violently; anda crowd of dangerous characters collected, and tried to rescue him. I forced him into a shop, and was obliged to hold the doors against them. The shopkeeper refused to send for a constable till I threatened him. He begged of me several times to let him go, saying he would not do it again.
JAMES KENDRICK. I am a constable. I was fetched, and found the prosecutor surrounded by a parcel of pickpockets, men, women, and boys. I took the prisoner in charge, and asked if he could not leave off his old tricks - he made no answer.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. The other people were as likely to take it as me.
GUILTY. Aged 13.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18230910-262]
1231. JANE JONES was indicted for stealing, on the 12th of July, six sovereigns, nine shillings, and a Foreign coin, called a Ducat, value 9 s. 6 d., the monies of Jacob Abrahams, from his person.
JACOB ABRAHAMS. I am a tailor, and live in Mary-le-bone-lane. On the 12th of July, at eight o'clock in the evening, I was returning from the City; I was not intoxicated. I went into a public-house in Holborn, for half a pint of beer; the prisoner stood by me - I asked the way to Mary-le-bone-lane; she asked me to treat her, and then she would show me the way - I paid for a glass of rum for her, and changed a sovereign to pay for it, and put the silver in my bag, which she saw me put into my fob - she then went out with me to shew me the way, and took me to a little dark street. I said that was not my way, for I must go along a broad street, and I would not go with her. She said that was the nearest way - I then followed her till she stopped at a door; she opened a room door, and said,
"Come in, come in;" I said I must go home - she said,
"Don't be frightened;" took me by the arm, and said,
"Come in, and I will shew you the way directly." I went, in, and found another woman in the room; she sent her out, and came close to me; I could not understand what she said. She put her hand into my watch pocket, and took my money out; my purse was above my watch; she said,
"I shall go and shut the door;" went out, and did not return. I got out of the room in about five minutes, and went into the public-house to inquire if they knew her. I am certain of her.
PHILIP RILEY. I am a patrol. On the Tuesday evening after the robbery, Abrahams described the prisoner to me. I went to the room where he said it happened; it is in Bainbridge-street - I found the prisoner in Maynard-street; the prosecutor came up; we took another woman, who he described as having been in the room - she is a common prostitute, her name is Keath - she said she was the woman who was in the room; but that the prisoner was not the person. He insisted that she was.
EDWARD YARRANTON. I was with Riley. The prosecutor saw the prisoner, and said positively she was the woman.
Prisoner's Defence. I never saw him till I was taken.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18230910-263]
Third Jury, Before W. Arabin, Esq.
1232. GEORGE SMITH was indicted for stealing, on the 24th of August, a seal, value 2 l.; a key, value 6 s., and a piece of ribbon, value 3 d., the goods of Walter Jones, from his person.
WALTER JONES. I am a labourer, and live with Mr. Taylor, in the City-road. On the 24th of August, about a quarter past eleven o'clock at night, I was passing through Finsbury-square; I was sober, but finding myself ill, I sat down at a door. The prisoner and two more came up, and said,
"Come, Jem, we are your friends, come with us;" they were strangers to me. I was in the act of getting up, and they snatched at my ribbon, which broke, and they got my seal and key, and ran across the square - I immediately ran after them, calling Stop thieves! and the patrol stopped the prisoner. I had never lost sight of him, and am certain he is the one who snatched at my watch. We took him towards the watch-house - he said he was innocent, and made a spring to get from the watchman; but I caught hold of him - an officer came to us. I have not found my seal.
Prisoner. Q. Do you say you were not intoxicated - A. Yes; I was coming from Greenwich; I had been at my dinner, but nothing after.
THOMAS HARRIS. I am a patrol. At a quarter to eleven o'clock, I heard a cry of Stop him! immediately turned round, and saw the prosecutor following the prisoner by the rails - I secured him, and in Chiswell-street, he tried to get from me. Jones appeared sober, and said he was the man who robbed him.
ELLEN RITCHIE. I was walking out about ten o'clock; the prosecutor was quite intoxicated, and dropped down his head - three persons took him by the arm, and said,
"Jem, get up, I will see you safe home." I thought they were all friends - two of them ran from the square, and this intoxicated man ran after them, and one went up the City-road.
Q. How came you to think he was intoxicated - A. Because he dropped his head down, and made no answer to them. I thought it was all a lark.
Q. Did he stagger as he ran - A. He seemed not himself - I cannot swear to the prisoner.
ISAAC NEWTON. I am a watchman of Chiswell-street, with my back toward the square. Somebody called me - I looked round, and saw three or four persons scufiling in the middle of the road, and found the prisoner trying to get from Harris. Jones laid hold of him; before we got on the pavement, he dropped down as if in a fit, and did not speak again - I believe it was sham. I found only 9 d. on him.
JACOB BRIDGES. I am a constable. I received him in charge. I saw Ritchie afterwards; she asked what I had done with the prisoner, and said she saw him take hold of the gentleman, and say,
"Jem, get up." Jones appeared sober.
Prisoner's Defence. On the 24th of August, after church, I went to my uncle's, and was returning. I had stopped late, and ran as hard as I could; and in the square I heard somebody halloo Stop him! I turned round, and the gentleman stopped me; they almost throttled me, and being faint with running I fell into a swoon. I never stop out late.
GUILTY. Aged 23.
Confined One Year.
[trialAccount t18230910-264]
1233. JOHN PRICE was indicted for stealing, on the 3rd of July, a necklace, value 3 s., and a piece of silver coin, value 4 d., the property of John Harris, from the pro- person of Sarah Harris, an infant.
HANNAH HARRIS. I am the wife of John Harris; we live in Ogle-street, Mary-le-bone. On the 3d of July, I was in Cleveland-street with my infant; about half-past six o'clock in the evening, looking at a fire, my child had a necklace round her neck, with a coin fastened to it. I saw the prisoner, with two or three others, following and looking at me, so that I went to a doorway to get from them. I noticed the prisoner particularly; he crept behind the child and touched my shoulder - I turned round, and he was in the act of unclasping the necklace. I said,
"You villain, are you taking my child's necklace" - he made no answer, but gave it a pull and got it - he snatched a piece of flesh off the child's neck, and gave the necklace to another man. I caught hold of him immediately, and held him; he was taken into a baker's shop, and the mob broke the shop window to get him out; but he was detained, and put into the watch-house.
Prisoner. Q. Did I not come out of the court with other people - A. No one was in the court.
CHARLOTTE ADNAM. I stood close to the prosecutrix - I knew her before - she called out - I saw the prisoner give the necklace to another man; she laid hold of him, and I ran for her husband. I am certain he is the man.
Prisoner's Defence. I stood in Hampstead-road with my basket; a gentleman bought some barley-sugar, and told me to take it to Cleveland-street; seeing a mob, I went down there and stood by a baker's shop; this woman came out of the court, and said,
"You have got my child's beads." I had my hands in my pocket, and said,
"If you think so, search me."
GUILTY. Aged 19.
Confined One Year.
[trialAccount t18230910-265]
1234. JOSEPH WILLIAMS was indicted for stealing, on the 10th of September, a watch, value 30 s., the goods of John Honghton.
KETORAH HOUGHTON. I am the wife of John Haughton. On the 10th of September, about half-past ten o'clock in the morning, I saw the prisoner coming out of the area, and tapped at the parlour window twice, and asked him what he wanted; he made no answer. I flew to the door and called out Stop that man! - he was stopped and brought back. I went into the kitchen and missed my watch, which was there three minutes before. It was not found on him. Smith produced it; he had no business in the area.
ANN SMITH. I was standing by the top of my place when the prisoner ran by me. The prosecutrix called out Stop that man! I saw him taken and brought back. I saw him take something from his pocket, and lay it on the window cill. I immediately said,
"Let us see what he has put there," and found it was the watch.
BENJAMIN PARROTT. I received him in charge with the watch.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I went down the area after a young man, whom I knew. I found it was the wrong place, and came up again. This woman called Stop thief! and I came back immediately.
GUILTY. Aged 18.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18230910-266]
1235. THOMAS THOMPSON was indicted for stealing, on the 20th of August, a handkerchief, value 2 s., the goods of William Brown, from his person.
WILLIAM BROWN. I am a half-pay captain. On the 20th of August, about a quarter before nine o'clock, I was walking in Gower-street, and turning into Bedford-square, I felt somebody at my pocket - turned round, and found the prisoner with my handkerchief in his hand. I took him across the square, and met Leonard, who took him - he had got it out of my pocket.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
GUILTY. Aged 13.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18230910-267]
1236. ELIZABETH SHEEN was indicted for stealing on the 28th of July, a set of bagpipes, value 2 l. 10 s., the goods of Martin Flannagan.
MARTIN FLANNAGAN. I play the bagpipes. On the 28th of July, about seven o'clock in the evening, I was at Kennedy's public-house, in St. James's-street, playing. I tied my bagpipes up in a bag, and put them on the table, and went into the yard for a minute or two, and on returning they were gone. I had left the prisoner in the room. She was also gone; I gave an alarm, and have never found them. She came into the house half an hour after; I asked her for them, she said she did not take them. I did not detain her - but she was apprehended afterwards.
ANN CRAWLEY. I was at the public-house, and saw the prosecutor there. There was a green parcel on the table; I did not know what it was; I saw the prisoner take it off the table and go out; but whether she took it out, I do not know; I mentioned it five or six days after. When I found Flannagan had lost his pipes, I told the landlady of it the same night; I was never taken up for it. I never saw her before.
JOHN GREEN. I am beadle. I apprehended the prisoner - I took Crawley to appear against her; she was not charged with the theft.
MARTIN FLANNAGAN. I do not remember seeing Crawley there.
Prisoner's Defence. This woman tells falsehoods; she sleept at my place every night.
ANN CRAWLEY. We both live in one house, but are not related.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18230910-268]

I missed a table spoon on the 3d of August, and found it in pawn; nine or ten days after, I took the prisoner, which was on the Wednesday after, I missed it; she denied ever seeing it. I found it in pawn - she had no business in my room.
JAMES BARNES. I am shopman to Messrs. Graham and Co., of Tothill-street. I have a spoon pawned by the prisoner, in the name of Mary Castle.

The prisoner pleaded Distress.

1240. GEORGE SOLLOWAY was indicted for stealing, on the 1st of July, a hat, value 3 s., and a handkerchief, value 1 s., the goods of Henry Howes.
HENRY HOWES. I am a coachman. On the 1st of July, I lodged at the Sportsman, public-house, High-street, Mary-le-bone. About one o'clock I took off my hat and put it on the tap-room table, with my handkerchief in it. I went out for half an hour, and on returning they were gone. Nobody was in the room when I went out. I found it before the Magistrate two days after. My hat was new.
JOSEPH PARKER. I am a pawnbroker, and live at Cambridge-heath. On the 1st of July the prisoner came about six o'clock in the evening, and offered to pawn a hat, which I refused, as it was wet. He went to the door, returned, and offered a handkerchief, which I took in in the name of Soloway.
HENRY HOWES. I have no mark on the handkerchief.
NOT GUILTY.

1241. JAMES SIMMS was indicted for stealing, on the 3d of September, 14 ozs. of tea, value 2 s., the goods of the United Company of Merchants trading to the East Indies .
SECOND COUNT, stating it to belong to persons unknown.
MR. WALFORD conducted the prosecution.
AARON SAUL. I am a labourer in the service of the East India Company. On the 3d of September the prisoner was employed to carry hoops to mend the chests. I saw him looking at several chests, and at last came to one which was very much broken. He stirred the tea up with a small stick, and put his right hand into the chest several times, and then into his pocket. I informed the elder.
WILLIAM GIBSON. I searched the prisoner. I could find nothing. He denied having anything. I made him unbutton his trowsers, and found he had a large pair of pantaloons under them, with a bag sown where the pockets should be, and there I found 14 ozs. of tea. The bag would not be felt in rubbing down. He had 16 s. 6 d. a week, and works from eight to two o'clock only. I have lost the bag.
Prisoner's Defence. It was not a bag; it was a pocket.
AARON SAUL. It was above a foot long.
GUILTY. Aged 41.
Recommended to Mercy. - Confined Three Months.

JUDY BROWN. I live in Rosemary-lane. On the 30th of March. I found a chest broken open, and two knives and five forks gone; four of them were silver handled; also two brushes and three tumblers. Powis used to come to the house to see his grandfather, but I never saw him after missing the things.
ABRAHAM ELLIS. I am a headborough. On the 7th of July, I took Powis, and asked what he did with the things he stole from Mrs. Brown - he said voluntarily, he would take me to James Knight 's where he had left them; we went and found another knife there, and two knives and five forks in the cupboard. I told her to bring her son to me when she came home, and she did in the evening.

On the 12th July, I was at the Duke of Bedford public-house, Old Gravel-lane, talking to Holt, and saw the prisoner enter the yard, and as he went out, he took these things off the line, put them under his waistcoat, and went into the tap-room - we went and took them from him.

[trialAccount t18230910-276]
1245. MARY STURCH was indicted for stealing, on the 8th of September, a purse, value 1 s.; a sovereign, and 17 s., the property of Charles Upstill.
ELIZABETH UPSTILL. I am wife of Charles Upstill; we live in St. Pancras; the prisoner lodged nearly a year in the back parlour, with her husband; my purse containing this money was in the front parlour cupboard - I put it there on the 8th of September; left the key in, and on the 9th missed it, and saw it on Wednesday evening - the sovereign was wrapped in a piece of coarse paper.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Did her father lodge with her - A. Yes, at first - he turned her out of doors, and we took her in; she was married at my house to Sturch; he was not an acquaintance of mine; I did nottell her father she was going to be married; the purse is a French one made of beads - I never said it was a steel purse; I cannot swear to the money, but swear to a piece of the flounce of my silk gown, which it was wrapped in; we found it concealed in a blacking bottle, and put down the privy.
SARAH UPSTILL. I am fourteen years old. On the 8th of September, my mother was out, I remained at home; the prisoner came into the room, and sent me for 2 lbs. of mutton, and when I returned, she stood at the door, talking to a man, and asked me to go to Somers-town. I said No, for my father would be home.
JOHN KATLEY. I lodge in the house; I advised the prosecutrix on Tuesday to send for an officer; the prisoner heard me advise her, and then went twice into the privy within the space of a minute and half. I afterwards saw Middleton find a blacking bottle in the privy; we broke it, and out fell a piece of black silk with a sovereign, fourteen shillings, and half-a-crown.
THOMAS MIDDLETON. I am a butcher. I found the blacking bottle among the soil on Tuesday morning; this money was in it wrapped in silk; the sovereign and fourteen shillings were wrapped in paper. I took it out, and gave it to Upstill.
JOHN LIMBRICK. I have the money and part of the blacking bottle - the prosecutrix gave me the purse.
ELIZABETH UPSTILL. Howes gave me the purse; I can swear to it among a thousand. I can swear to the paper the sovereign is in, for I broke it off a sugar bag.
Cross-examined. Q. Will you swear to a piece of paper - A. I have the piece I tore it off of.
SARAH HOWSE. I am cook to Mr. Bailey, of Meclenburgh-square. On Monday evening, the 8th of September, the prisoner gave me this purse, as a keep-sake, and desired me not to say any thing to Sally Upstill. When I heard of the robbery, I mentioned it.
MR. PHILIPS to ELIZABETH UPSTILL. Q. On your oath, was the half-crown found in that bottle - Yes.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18230910-277]
1246. WILLIAM SMITH and JOHN WATERS were indicted for stealing, on the 12th of August, a looking-glass, value 5 s.; a tea-tray, value 2 s., and a trunk, value 2 s., the goods of Thomas Horning.
JOHN MURRAY. I am a watchman of Ratcliff. On the 29th of August, at four o'clock in the morning, I stopped the prisoners in company together in Rose-lane: Waters had the tea-tray and looking-glass, and Smith a trunk; Smith said, they got them from on board of ship; Waters did not contradict him. I secured them, opened the box, and found out the prosecutor who claimed it; he lived at Limehouse.
THOMAS HORNING. I am a publican. This property is mine; I left it in care of Mr. Trist.
WILLIAM TRIST. I live in Gunn-lane, Lime-house. This property was left in my care; I put them in my loft, and missed them on the 12th, and found a window open, which had been shut at night.
SMITH - GUILTY. Aged 16.
WATERS - GUILTY. Aged 17.
Recommended to Mercy. - Confined Two Months.

1247. STEPHEN ABBOTT and JOSEPH M'NAIR were indicted for stealing, on the 2d of September, a box, value 3 s., the goods of Jacob Temple; and fourteen books, value 20 s.; two table-cloths, value 3 s., and eight napkins, value 2 s., the goods of Thomas Richard Barker.
THOMAS HEARMAND. I am inspector of the watch, and live in Fashion-street, Spital-fields. On the 2d of September, between three and four o'clock in the afternoon, the prisoners passed my window; Abbott had a box, and M'Nair was behind him, in his company. I followed them about a hundred yards; M'Nair took a cap off Abbott's head, and took the box from him; I stopped them, and asked whose box it was - M'Nair said it was his own - Abbott heard it, and said nothing. I took them both into custody. It contained books and linen.
SAMUEL GREEN. I am a watchman - I was with Hearmand - his account is correct.
JACOB TEMPLE. I am an upholsterer, and live in Bedford-place, Commercial Road. On the 2d of September Thomas Barker sent me these books to pack up, which I did, and put them outside the door - and missed them at half-past two o'clock. Fashion-street is half a mile off.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
M'NAIR'S Defence. A sailor gave me the box to carry from Ratcliffe High-way to Finsbury-square. I met Abbott and asked him to give me a lift.
ABBOTT - GUILTY. Aged 18.
Confined Two Months.
M'NAIR - GUILTY. Aged 18.
Transported for Seven Years.

1248. THOMAS DAVIS was indicted for stealing, on the 8th of August, seven saws, value 20 s.; two planes, value 4 s.; five chisels, value 3 s.; a rule, value 2 s.; a bevil, value 2 s., and a pair of compasses, value 1 s., the goods of John Lloyd.
WILLIAM FULLBROOK. I am a patrol of Hackney. On the 8th of August, at four o'clock in the morning, I was in Hackney Marsh; I saw the prisoner walking backward and forward by the river, with something on his back - I went to him and asked what it was - he said his own tools - that he came from High Wycombe, and would answer no more questions. I found a basket on his back containing these tools.
JOHN GARVA. The prisoner was brought to the watch-house with the tools; he could give no account of himself.
THOMAS FURLEY. I am apprentice to Mr. John Lloyd of Hommerton. I know these tools very well. They were taken from the work-shop on the 8th of August. The tiles were taken off the roof, and the laths cut away.
GUILTY. Aged 23.
Confined One Year.
[trialAccount t18230910-280]
FOURTEENTH DAY, THURSDAY, SEPTEMBER 24.
Middlesex Cases, Third Jury. Before W. Arabin, Esq.
1249. JOSHUA TAYLOR was indicted for stealing, on the 9th of September, six yards of flannel, value 6 s., the goods of Edward Roberts.
JOHN PORTER. I am servant to Edward Roberts, linen-draper. On the 9th of September, between seven and eight o'clock in the evening, the prisoner came to my master's shop. A pane of glass was broken, and the flannel drawn through the glass. Lane alarmed me; I ran out, and, at the corner of Prince's-street, found the prisoner in custody.
JOSEPH LANE. I was near the shop. I live in Queen-street, Oxford-street. I saw the prisoner drawing the flannel through the hole in the window. He ran off. I followed, and saw him stopped; he dropped it immediately.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I met a boy in Oxford-street, who stopped at the shop, and gave the flannel into my hand; half of it being out; he then threw it down, and I picked it up.
GUILTY. Aged 15.
Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18230910-281]

THOMAS ANKINS. I am a plaisterer, and live in Red Lion-street, Spitalfields. On the 3d of September, I had five chairs at my door, to be sold. I saw the prisoner. about one hundred yards off, with one of them, trying to hide it with her gown. I went up and took her. She said nothing, but appeared intoxicated.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
GUILTY. Aged 35.

1251. FRANCIS TURNER was indicted for stealing, on the 10th of September, a saw, value 5 s., and a rule, value 2 s., the goods of Joseph Gosbell; and a saw, value 5 s., the goods of John Birchmore.
WILLIAM TARRANT. I am a carpenter. On the 10th of September, I was in Felton-street, Hoxton, and saw the prisoner in some buildings in Harvey-street. I went to the house, and took him on the staircase, with two saws in his hand. He got up on the floor. I asked what business he had there. He threw the saws down, and jumped out of the first floor window. I ran round and caught him. He denied having anything else, but as we went along he dropped a rule, which I picked up, and delivered to Gosbell.
JOSEPH GOSBELL. I am a carpenter. I was employed in this house. The rule and one of the saws are mine. I found the saw in the well-hole.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
GUILTY. Aged 38.

1252. EDWARD BLUETT was indicted for embezzlement.
WILLIAM WALKER GILBERTSON. I am a baker, and live in Upper George-street, Bryanstone-square. The prisoner was in my service for six weeks, and employed to deliver bread and receive the money. Mr. Sagden owed me 9 s. 3 d., for which the prisoner never accounted. He left me, without notice, on the 13th of August, and was taken ten days after.
SAMUEL SAGDEN. I owed the prosecutor 9 s. 3 d., which I paid - I cannot say whether I paid the prisoner himself, or sent my servant to do it; but I produce the bill and receipt.
MR. GILBERTSON. This is in the prisoner's writing.
GUILTY. Aged 18.
Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18230910-284]
1253. MARY RYELL was indicted for stealing, on the 6th of September, twenty-three yards of cotton, value 20 s., the goods of John Augustus Brook, privately in his shop.
PETER KING. I am apprentice to John Augustus Brooks, a linen-draper, of Parliament-street. On the 6th of September, between three or four o'clock in the afternoon, the prisoner came to the shop, to match a pattern of blue printed cotton - I said we had none, and as she went out, I saw a piece of printed cotton under her arm, which had laid on a pile inside the door. I followed her into another shop, and as she came out, I asked her for the print that was under her arm - she said,
"Take it, and let me go, see how distressed I am." I gave her in charge.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
GUILTY. Aged 32.
Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18230910-285]
Before Mr. Recorder.
1254. JOHN HANISEL was indicted for stealing, on the 10th of September, a watch, value 3 l. 10 s., the goods of John James Harris, in his dwelling-house; and JACOB LEVY was indicted for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing it to be stolen.
JOHN JAMES HARRIS. I rent a house in Upper Nightingale-lane, Bishopsgate without, and am a watchmaker. On the 10th of September, my shopman came to inform me that a watch was stolen - I examined, and missed a customer's watch off my shop board. I gave information the afternoon after it was stolen. Hanisel was brought to my house, about two o'clock - I immediately went in pursuit of Levy; I met him in East Smithfield, collared him, and told him there was a valuable watch stolen from my shop, by Hanisel, his lodger, and he had received it from him, and given him 8 s. for it - he protested solemnly that he never saw it. He was brought before Hanisel, at my house, and said,
"Oh! you wicked youth, for charging me with the robbery, you know I know nothing of it, I have received no watch from you at all, and did not know you had one." Hanisel answered,
"Oh! Levy, you know you waited at the window, while I took the watch out, and you gave me 8 s. for it when I brought it out, and said it was an old watch, and worth no more;" he still denied all knowledge of it. The servant came in, and said she saw Levy at the window - he at last said,
"I wish you would let me speak to Brant;" he went into the kitchen with him; then came out, and said,
"I have got the watch from the prisoner, but I gave him 30 s. for it;" 3 l. 10 s. is a very low value for it - I should sell it to a dealer for that. I went with Levy and Brant, and he gave me up the watch.
Prisoner LEVY. Q. It was not me that gave you the watch - A. He sent a Jew to fetch it, and when he brought it, told him to give it to me.
HENRY BRANT. On the 11th of September, Harris sent for me, and asked if I knew anything of Hanisel - I went down to Blackwall, and then I came up again. I foundHanisel in Levy's house, in Ratcliff-highway, and told him he was suspected of stealing Harris's watch; I took him with me - Harris said,
"Where is my watch?" he said,
"You may overhaul me if you like, I have not got it." I searched him. He then said he had sold the watch to Levy - I went and took Levy, who denied the charge; he was brought to the shop; Hanisel said,
"I sold it to you for 8 s." Levy afterwards told me to go to a public-house, in Ratcliff-highway.
HANNAH REDGRAVE. I am servant to the prosecutor. On the 10th of September I saw Hanisel come into the shop and go out again; I did not see him take anything. I saw Levy looking in at the window at the time - I am sure of him. Hanisel wentout, and pointed to somebeads in the window - Levy met him at the window when he went out; Hanisel then came into the shop again. Levy then went away; I cannot say where he went to. Hanisel went out again in about half a minute, and the watch was missed. I saw both the prisoners at my master's next day, and told Levy I saw him standing at the window when Hanisel was in the shop the first time - he denied it.
Prisoner LEVY. Q. What time was it - A. Between twenty minutes and a quarter past four o'clock.
THOMAS HARRISON. I am shopman to Mr. Harris. About a quarter past four o'clock, Hanisel came in, and asked to look at some beads - I shewed him one row, which did not suit; he went out, and pointed to a particular row in the window, then came in, and I shewed them to him - he said he thought they would do, and he would go and fetch his woman; he did not ask about any watch. I saw nobody at the window. He went away, and in about a minute I missed the watch. I went out, but he was gone; he was brought to the shop next day, about two o'clock - I am certain of his person. Levy was charged with receiving it, and denied it at first; the girl said he was outside the window - he said it was false. Hanisel afterwards shewed me how he took it; he said he put his handkerchief over it, and took it up in it, and took it outside to Levy, who said at last, that he had bought it of him for 30 s.
THOMAS HARRISON. I am a constable, and took Hanisel - he said Levy only gave him 8 s. for it.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
HANISEL'S Defence. (Through an Intrepreter.) I lodge at Levy's. Brant tied my hands together, and beat me, and I confessed that I took it - distress and hunger drove me to it. He beat me, and made me say I sold it to Levy.
HENRY BRANT. I did not beat him; he kicked me, and I gave him a smack in the face.
LEVY'S Defence. The prisoner came to me on Tuesday night, in new clothes, and said he had sold his watch for 28 s., in Rosemary-lane. When the officer came, I said I knew the person who bought it of him, and took them to the man, who delivered it up.
HANISEL - GUILTY. Aged 17.
Sentence of Death recorded, but not passed.
LEVY - GUILTY. Aged 55.
Transported for Fourteen Years.
[trialAccount t18230910-286]
1255. WILLIAM WEAL was indicted for stealing, on the 12th of September, a cage, value 5 s., the goods of William Craghill.
WILLIAM CRAGHILL. I keep the Greyhound, public-house, in Old-street . My cage hung in the parlour, with a bird in it. I saw it safe twenty minutes before it was stolen - the window was open - it hung in the centre of the room - they must have got into the room to take it - the prisoner was brought back with it ten minutes after - he had a handkerchief over it, which he claimed.
HENRY HERBERT. I stood opposite the side of the house. I was informed a man had come out of the window. I looked and saw the prisoner twenty yards off with the cage; he threw it down when I got near him. I brought him back.
JOSEPH HARDING. I saw him come out of the window; had another boy with him, who was sitting on these at under the window. I saw the cage on the table in the parlour. I afterwards saw him throw it down, and picked it up.
THOMAS BENNETT. I am a constable. The cage was delivered to me; he claimed the handkerchief.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
GUILTY. Aged 19.
Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18230910-287]
1256. ELIZABETH WILLIAMS was indicted for stealing, on the 9th of August, 5 lbs. of bacon, value 3 s., the goods of Thomas Peters.
JOHN OXMAN. On the 9th of August, I was servant to Mr. Peters, cheesemonger, Whitecross-street. On Saturday night, about eleven o'clock, somebody told me this bacon was taken from the window. I ran out and saw the prisoner putting it into her apron, and I secured her with it. She keeps a bad house. The watchman found another piece in her apron, which she also said she took from the window.
JOHN BROWN. I am a watchman. I found another piece in her apron.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
GUILTY. Aged 43.

[trialAccount t18230910-288]
1257. THOMAS WILSON was indicted for stealing, on the 23d of August, a coat, value 3 l.; a pair of trowers, value 1 l.. and a handkerchief, value 5 s., the goods of William Neil.
WILLIAM NEIL. I live in Starr-street, Edgware-road, and am a labourer. On the 23rd of August, these things were in my room. I saw them safe about four o'clock in the afternoon, when I went out. I returned about six, and they were gone out of my box, which was locked. I found it unlocked. The prisoner was brought about six o'clock with them; he was a stranger; it is an unfinished house; we were working there.
EDWARD NEIL. I am a labourer. I was at work in a field. Maddox hallooed to me, and said the prisoner had stolen some of my clothes. I pursued and stopped him - my brother's things were picked up in a field; he was charged with stealing them, and begged for mercy. I stopped him a quarter of a mile from this house.
Prisoner. Q. Did I not stop voluntarily - A. I said,
"It is no use to run; I shall catch you, and then he stopped."
PATRICK MADDOX. I was at work near this house, and saw the skirt of the prisoner's coat going up stairs. I went round to the front of the house and stopped until he came out with a bundle. He said,
"Well, my man, is Mr.Gardner here?" I said, No; but he would be in a few minutes. He said,
"Tell your master a man over at the Star and Garter, public-house, wants to see him." Instead of going to the Star and Garter, he returned into the fields, and looked back to see if he was watched. It struck me directly that he was a thief, and I went after him. When I got near, he dropped the bundle, which contained Neil's clothes. I saw him stopped. Neil said he had stolen a waistcoat which he had lost before. He said,
"Oh! forgive me, and I will make it good."
GEORGE HANSON. I received him in charge, and found a skeleton key in the sleeve of his coat. It opened the room from which the things were stolen. He claimed the handkerchief they were wrapped in.
GUILTY. Aged 24.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18230910-289]
1238. JOHN MANLEY was indicted for stealing, on the 11th of March, fifteen dozen of willow squares, value 4 l.; and a bag, value 2 s., the goods of Walter Thomas.
WALTER THOMAS. I am a willow weaver, and live at No. 6, New-street, Shoreditch. The prisoner lived with me three or four months. I sent him with these squares to Mr. Grape, Clifton-street, Finsbury, to be dyed, on the 11th of March, between nine and ten o'clock. I did not see him again until he was taken, which was on the 11th of September. I found they had not been delivered to the dyer - they were worth 4 l. 2 s. 6 d.
JAMES GRAPE. I am a dyer; the prisoner never brought me these squares.
Prisoner's Defence. I never received these. I had some to deliver to Mr. Welch, which I did.
MARTHA BUCKLEY. I am servant to Mr. Thomas, and saw the squares delivered to the prisoner.
GUILTY. Aged 21.
Confined One Year.
[trialAccount t18230910-290]
1259. JOHN JONES was indicted for stealing, on the 6th of August, a half-crown, the money of John Brown, his master.
JOHN BROWN. I am a linen-draper, and live at Shadwell; the prisoner was in my service about three months. On the 6th of August, a cutler marked all the money in the tills on the head side, opposite to the face. About eleven o'clock at night, I gave 17 s. to a witness to put in the till, having myself first counted it; there were five half-crowns among it. The shutters were then shut, but the drawers not closed - no more customers came. It was his duty to empty the till at night - he did not know that I had done it. He came and separated the halfpence from the silver as usual - and in three or four minutes I came from outside the door with the witness, and immediately counted the silver, and found a half-crown missing. I accused him of taking it - he denied it. I found it in his pocket with other silver, and after some time he acknowledged taking it.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Did you know what the till contained - A. I might have left 1 s. in it, among the copper. I found only four half-crowns left. I said if his friends came forward, I would let him go. I wanted to know how much he had taken, as he acknowledged he had been robbing me ever since he came. I bought his friends might persuade him to tell me.
THOMAS HART. I was fellow shopman with the prisoner. Between ten and eleven o'clock, Mr. Brown gave me some money, which he said was marked - I put it into the till, when the silver was taken out at night; the prisoner was not in the shop at the time. The 17 s. consisted of half-crowns, and other coin. The shop was shut, and no customer came afterwards. The prisoner came in about five minutes after, and in three or four minutes the half-crown was missed. My master searched him, and found it on him among other money.
Cross-examined. Q. Did your master say he would let him go if his friends came forward - A. He said, if he would say where his friends were, he would send for them. I saw no violence used to him. I saw him laying on his back, he was handcuffed before the officer came.
WILLIAM SPOONER. I am a constable, and was sent for, and found him handcuffed - the money was delivered to me.
JOHN BROWN. Here is the half-crown, it is marked P. H.
WILLIAM CATTEN. I am a cutler. I marked this halfcrown for Brown.
Cross-examined. Q. You were present when this charge was made - A. Yes; Brown said, if he had friends, he would write to them - he said he had none.
GUILTY. Aged 21.
Confined Three Weeks.

JOHN BATES. The sheets were not let to the prisoner with the lodging.

1261. WILLIAM HELLENDON was indicted for stealing, on the 9th of August, a handkerchief, value 5 s., the goods of James Riley, from his person.
JAMES RILEY. I am a coal meter, and live in Lambeth-street. On the 9th of August, I was in Jerusalem-passage, Clerkenwell, between twelve and one o'clock; somebody spoke to me - I felt and missed my handkerchief; the person pointed to the prisoner; I seized him, he denied taking it. I took him to a constable, who searched him, but could not find it; it was a large blue one, with white spots.
JOHN BRYAN. I am a lapidary, and was in Jerusalem passage, and saw the prisoner with another young man behind Riley; I saw the prisoner put his hand into Riley's pocket, and pull out a large blue silk handkerchief - he was about twenty yards from me; he gave it to his companion, who ran off with it immediately. I told Riley, he secured the prisoner, but the other got away.
JOHN BALLARD. I am a constable. Riley gave charge of the prisoner, but I found nothing on him.
Prisoner's Defence. I saw a man rush by me, a man came up and said, I was concerned with him, but I am totally innocent.
GUILTY. Aged 18.
Recommended to Mercy. - Fined 1 s. and Discharged.
[trialAccount t18230910-293]
1262. JAMES GREEN was indicted for stealing, on the 6th of July, two shirts, value 4 s.; five waistcoats, value 10 s., and five pair of trowsers, value 1 l., the goods of Ann Thompson.
ANN THOMPSON. I keep a sale shop in Rosemary-lane. On the 6th of July, I missed a waistcoat from the door, the prisoner was my shopman. I charged him with it - he denied it. I sent for a constable, who found some duplicates on him, and a shirt in his hat; I found the other things in pawn.
ABRAHAM ELLIS. I am a constable. I was sent for, and found a shirt in his hat, and four duplicates on him.
STEPHEN JAMES SWENEY. I am shopman to Mr. Fleming, pawnbroker, Whitechapel. I have two pair of trowsers, a shirt, and waistcoat, pawned at different times; I did not take them in.
ROBERT LINWOOD. I am a pawnbroker. I have a pair of trowsers and a waistcoat pawned, on the 28th of June, in the name of John Brown, New Road. The prisoner pawned them I am certain.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
The prisoner pleaded distress.
GUILTY. Aged 34.
Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18230910-294]
1263. JOHN FRANKLIN was indicted for stealing, on the 11th of August, a handkerchief, value 5 s., the goods of John Newman, from his person.
JOHN NEWMAN, JUN. I live in Old Swan-lane, and am clerk to Messrs. Wigan and Co. On the 11th of August, about nine o'clock in the evening, I was in Crown-street, Finsbury, with my handkerchief in my pocket, twisted round my pocket-book. I felt something in my pocket, turned round, and saw my handkerchief pass from the prisoner to his two companions; I seized him immediately - the other two went away towards Bishopsgate-street - I have not found it. I took him to the watch-house.
Cross-examined by MR. PLATT. Q. You were walking with two young ladies I believe - A. Yes; one in each arm. I think I saw him about twenty yards below, before he robbed me.
ELIZABETH EASTWOOD. I was with Mr. Newman, and saw the prisoner take his hand out of Mr. Newman's pocket - but could not see what was in it. There were two others with him. Mr. Newman took the prisoner immediately.
Cross-examined. Q. You were looking before you I suppose - A. I turned round immediately as Mr. Newman did - he steped suddenly - I saw him take his hand out of the pocket - but saw no handkerchief in it.
Prisoner's Defence. I was walking the same way as the prosecutor - he turned round suddenly, and said I picked his pocket - I said I had not, and went quietly to the watch-house; if guilty, is it reasonable that I should have gone so quietly.
GUILTY. Aged 20.
Confined One Year.

JOHN WRIGHT. I was with Osborne, and saw the prisoner take up the bedstead and carry it away. We secured him.

1266. ELIZABETH ANDREWS was indicted for stealing, on the 28th of August, a seal, value 15 s., and a veil, value 5 s., the goods of John Marsden.
MARY MARSDEN. I am the wife of John Marsden; we live in Great Warner-street; I hired the prisoner to nurse for a few days. On the 28th of August I missed the property. The seal was affixed to a watch which hung over the fire-place; the veil was in the drawer not locked. I found it in pawn next day; I told her she had taken them she said if I would allow her to go, she would get them for me. I have not found the seal.
GEORGE HARVEY. I am servant to a pawnbroker, and live in Cross Turnmill-street; I have a veil, which I saw the prisoner pawn. Another girl was with her.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
THOMAS MARKHAM. I took her in charge; she said she had pawned the veil, and given the seal to George Mumford, who has absconded.
GUILTY. Aged 16.

1268. THOMAS COLLECHER was indicted for stealing, on the 11th of September, a jacket, value 10 s.; a pair of trowsers, value 10 s., and a pair of drawers, value 1 s., the goods of Frederick Johnson.
FREDERICK JOHNSON. I am a seaman. The prisoner lodged in the same room as myself, at St. Catherine's, with three others. My jacket hung on a nail in the bed-room, and the other clothes were locked up in my chest. I missed them, and found the prisoner in Ratcliff-highway with them in his hand. He said they were not mine. I had him secured. He then said he had broken the lock open to get them.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I gave 1 s. 6 d. for them in the street three days before.
JAMES ELLIOTT. I lodge in this room. I heard the prisoner say he broke the box open, and took them.
GUILTY. Aged 22.
Confined Six Weeks.
[trialAccount t18230910-300]
1269. WILLIAM WILLIAMS was indicted for stealing, on the 25th of June, a waistcoat, value 2 s., the goods of Francis Sanders.
FRANCIS SANDERS. On the 25th of June I was in the Black Lion, public-house, Castle-street, Bloomsbury. My waistcoat was in my hat on the seat. I was having a pint of beer. I went into the back-yard, leaving it there. The prisoner was sitting in the same box. A boy came into the yard, and I missed it out of the hat, and the prisoner was gone. I went after him, and saw him five days after by St. Giles's church. He confessed it.
GEORGE LEE. I am a pawnbroker. On the 25th of June, between six and seven o'clock, the prisoner pawned the waistcoat for 1 s. 6 d.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
GUILTY. Aged 17.
Fined One Shilling and Discharged.
[trialAccount t18230910-301]
1270. JOHN CLARK and JOHN JOHNSON were indicted for stealing, on the 8th of September, two handkerchiefs, value 7 s., the goods of Vincent Cross.
VINCENT CROSS. I am a linen-draper, and live in Kingsland-road. These handkerchiefs laid in my window. I missed them, jumped over the counter, and seeing Johnson in the shop, I seized him. Both prisoners were in the shop, but Johnson was nearest the window. I observed him throw the handkerchiefs from him. Clark remained in the shop. They had asked for two black silk laces, and tendered me a 6 d., for which I gave change.
WILLIAM SMITH. I am a constable. On the 8th of September I took charge of the prisoner. I found the laces in Clark's hand. Neither of them had a farthing about them, although the prosecutor had given Clark 3 d.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
JOHNSON'S Defence. I never had them. He searched me, and not finding them, said I had thrown them over the counter.
CLARK'S Defence. I was not with Johnson.
JOHNSON - GUILTY.
Transported for Seven Years.
CLARK - NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18230910-302]
1271. HENRY MEAKINS was indicted for stealing, on the 1st of September, a lamp-shade, value 2 s., the goods of George Woolford.
GEORGE WOOLFORD. I am the Blackwall carrier. On the 1st of September, between seven and eight o'clock in the evening, this lamp-shade was tied to the tail of my cart, which was going on between Whitechapel and Commercial-road. I had a parcel to deliver; and going round the cart, found the cord cut, and the shade gone, with a prickle which it was in. Jones brought the prisoner to me with it a week after; it was safe in Leadenhall-street. The burner was in the basket with it. I bought it of Messrs. Pellatt and Co.
WILLIAM JONES. I am a patrol of Bow-street. On the 1st of September I was in Gulston-street, and observed the prisoner coming along with this glass-shade in his hand, done up in brown paper. I asked what he had there. He said a glass bowl, and that it was made for him by one Abrahams, of Wheeler-street, Spitalfields, and he gave 7 s. 6 d. for it. I sent to Abrahams, and in consequence of what he said, I detained the prisoner, and found the prosecutor out a week afterwards. He had not got the prickle.
FREDERICK MOSS. I am mounter to Messrs. Pellatt and Co. On the 1st of September I sold the lamp, with this shade to Woolford. I know it to be the same.
Prisoner's Defence. I bought it for 7 s. 6 d.
GUILTY.
Transported for Seven Years.

Fourth Jury, Before W. Arabin, Esq.
1272. WILLIAM HOOPER was indicted for stealing, on the 25th of August, three spoons, value 6 s., the goods of John Edbury.
JOHN PARTRIDGE. I am beadle of Whitechapel. On the 25th of August I was in Rosemary-lane, and saw the prisoner offering three spoons for sale to a Jew. I asked him where he got them from. He said from Mrs. Noon, of Ailiff-street. I took him in custody with them, and found out the prosecutrix.
Cross-examined by MR. WALFORD. Q. He was not sober, was he - A. No.
MARY EDBURY. I am the wife of John Edbury, and live in Little Aliff-street. The prisoner was recommended to me as a lodger, and was one night in my house. I did not miss the spoons until they were brought to me. They were taken off the back-room table. He was at my house a quarter of an hour before.
Cross-examined. Q. He might have taken them in a joke - A. Very likely.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. She sent me out for rum, and I do not know whether they were put into my pocket or not, for I never saw them.
GUILTY. Aged 21.
Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18230910-304]
1273. HENRY HOLDER was indicted for stealing, on the 30th of July, twenty-four lbs. of soap, value 14 s., the goods of Joseph Mote, his master.
MR. BRODRICK conducted the prosecution.
JOSEPH MOTE. I am a soap manufacturer, and live in Hill-street, Finsbury. The prisoner was my confidential clerk for three years. After soap is manufactured, it is put into the store-room; in consequence of suspicion, I employed two officers to watch: he occupied two rooms in my house. On the 30th of July, I ascertained the quantity of soap in the store-room. I reckoned 38 cakes to a cwt., and by that calculation, there were 2 l cwt.; I ordered him to deliver 6 1/4 cwt. to a customer; only a labourer, myself, and him had access to the soap. The labourers were under his controul. The soap was moist on the evening of the 29th - it was then all correct; he was to deliver out the 6 1/4 cwt. On the 30th, the officers took him into custody; on that day I went with them into his room; I found eight cakes of soap on the floor, between the bed and wall; it was moist, similar to the pile; I went to the store-room, and found eight cakes deficient.
Cross-examined by MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. When had the pile been manufactured - A. I cannot exactly say; I think not a fortnight or three weeks - there might be two or three tons weight made. I sold Mr. Freeman 56 lbs. of each; the prisoner said it was the residue of a 1/4 of cwt. he had bought of Freeman; he bought soap of me occasionally; there was a closet in his room, where he might have hid it. I had Freeman apprehended.
MR. BRODRICK. Q. He was charged with receiving the soap - A. Yes; soap in small quantities will get dry in a quarter of an hour.
JOSEPH HANDLEY. I was directed to watch the prisoner and took him in charge on Wednesday, the 30th of July; and told him under what circumstances I came, and asked him if I should find any soap there? He said I should; he has two rooms over the warehouse. He said, he bought the soap of Freeman; I found eleven cakes piled together, between the bed and wall - the bed was two feet from the wall - it resembled the pile in size, appearance, and moisture.
Prisoner's Defence. They went to inquire if I had it of Freeman; I said, I had the soap, it was part of a 1/4 of cwt. which I bought the week preceding of Freeman; I was detained while the prosecutor went to inquire at Freeman's.
JOSEPH MOTE. I had not sold soap to him, since he lived on the premises, but think two years ago he bought a little.
FREEMAN. I am a tallow-chandler, and live in Wilson-street, Finsbury. On the 23d of July, I bought 1/4 a cwt. of soap of Mote; and on the 25th, sold the prisoner a 1/4 of cwt. of it. I believe the soap to be part of the same.
MR. BRODRICK. Q. He was servant to a soap-maker - A. Yes, I have sold him several 1/4 cwts.; I charged him 2 s. more on a cwt. than I gave; I do not sell soap for less than I give. I remember offering a cwt. at 1 s. less, when I wanted money to make up a bill. I will not swear that I have not sold some to Cook, of Union-street. I never sold soap to a man named Cook.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18230910-305]
1275. JOHN FEARBY and JOSEPH WOOD were indicted for stealing, on the 12th of September, five sovereigns; one 10 l., and one 5 l., Bank notes, the property of Thomas Herrington.
THOMAS HERRINGTON. I am a farmer's bailiff. I left my situation in Hertfordshire, a week ago last Monday; and on Friday, between nine and ten o'clock in the morning, I was going to Smithfield market; the prisoner Wood overtook me, and asked me if I was going to the market; I said, I was; he said he was going there too, and asked which was the best market-day; I said, I rather thought Monday; he said, he was come to town on business, and thought he would stop to see Monday's market; we walked nearly through the market, he asked me to take a pint of beer; I said, with all my heart, I was going to follow him into the house, but he said, No, we will sit down outside; he went in and fetched a pint of beer, we sat down and drank it. I said, I would be a pint, but we had no more; he carried the pot in and paid for it; he then said, we would walk on further, if I had no objection, and then have another pint; we went through the market, then walked away till we came into the New Road - he asked me to go in there and have a pint - I said Yes, with all my heart - and so we went in. A young man was there, who was afterwards with the prisoners. Fearby came in while we sat there. They seemed all strangers to each other. Fearby said he was a countryman, and had come to settle some business of money that had been owing for sixteen months - that he had applied to a gentleman, who he mentioned, who got 550 l. for him, and 550 l. for his brother - and that his brother was very infortunate in getting into a coach, he fell down and put his instep out. Wood said there was no such a word as infortunate, and wanted to lay a wager about it - they laid a pot of beer - Fearby paid for it, and then said we all three looked like poor men, and could not shew 10 l. a-piece. I said I had not 10 l. with me, but I expected some up from home by that day's post, and could produce it in a short time. Fearby then wanted to lay 3 l. to 1 l. that we did not each produce a 10 l. note by one o'clock. Wood and the young man pulled out their 10 l. each; and the young man walked with me up to Tottenham-court-road, where my parcel was coming, but the letter had not come. We left the prisoners at the public-house. Fearby then said he would give me till two o'clock - I said I would stand to the wager. The young man said he wanted to go into Tottenham-court-road to get change for some money, and would go with me; the prisoners said they would go too; we all four of us went; and they went into an inn while I went for my letter - it was not come - I asked my friend to lend me 20 l., which he did. We then went back to the inn, and shewed Fearby our 10 l. each, all three of us; then he said he would lay another 1 l. that we could not shew another 10 l. I then shewed him another 10 l.; he then gave into the hands of Wood a 5 l. note, as he said they then wanted to go and see the balloon go off - and so did I. They got a coach, and we all went in it; the young man looked out of the coach, and said he knew of a convenient place where we could have refreshment. Fearby had said before that he would treat us with a bottle of wine, and change the 5 l. note; so we went to the inn. Wood and the young man asked if they might go up to a private room - the landlord said we might have the parlour - and we went into the parlour. Wood called for a pot of ale and a glass of shrub and water - and the young man said he would go and order some steaks, and while they were cooking we could have a game at cards. They asked me to play - I said No. Fearby and the young man played three or four games at all-fours; the young man had four chalks out of five. Fearby dealt the last deal, and begged to be excused to go out for a moment after he had dealt his cards, and wished us not to look at them. The young man took up his hand of cards, looked at them, and said it was such a good hand, he was sure to beat. Wood said
"Let us lay him a wager about the game;" so when Fearby came in, the young man said he would lay him a wager - oh, he said, he would lay him any thing - that he would beat either 10 l. or 20 l. a-piece if he liked; then Fearby said he would lay 60 l. to our 20 l. each; he got up and put his money in a hat on another table - the others put down theirs, and wished me to put down mine - so I did, into the hat with the others; they then wanted me to play the hand for him, and said play how I would I was sure to win; I said I would not, three or four times - but they very much persuaded me, saying I could not lose - and so I played and lost - then Fearby said he had won the game, and got up and took the hat. Wood beckoned me out of the door - I went; and he said he should be back in ten minutes or a quarter of an hour, if I could step with him; and when I returned into the room they were all gone. I went to Hatton-garden, and they were taken up that evening.
Cross-examined by MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. You understand all-fours, don't you - A. No; very little; but they said play how I would, I was sure to win.
Q. You laid your money into the hat, to be won or lost as the game should go - A. Yes; I lost the game.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18230910-306]
1276. MARY EDWARDS was indicted for stealing, on the 4th of August, two half-crowns, the monies of John Steins, from his person.
JOHN STEINS. I am a journeyman tailor. On the 4th of August, between three and four o'clock in the afternoon, I was walking in the Hampstead-road; the prisoner came up and asked me for some halfpence - she had two little girls with her. She followed me a long while, and said they had nothing to eat - and I said I had no halfpence. I put my hand into my pocket, pulled out my money - I had some silver and copper together; the prisoner snatched two half-crowns out of my hand and ran off. I followed and overtook her in five minutes, without losing sight of her; she had not ran far; I took one half-crown from her hand, but could not find the other. I did not see her searched, but the other was found upon her - I was sober - I knew nothing of her before.
CHARLES COUSINS. I am an officer. I searched the prisoner, and found the half-crown in her hand. She said he gave it her.
Prisoner's Defence. I never said any such thing.
GUILTY. Aged 15.
Strongly Recommended to Mercy. - Judgment Respited.

1278. JOHN COX was indicted for stealing, on the 2d of September, a glass domb, value 6 s. the goods of James Smeehart.
HORATIO TAYLOR. I am shopman to Mr. James Smeehurst, lamp-manufacturer, New Bond-street. The prisoner was in his service.
THOMAS CLEMENTS. I am an officer. On the 2d of September, a little past nine o'clock at night, I was in Bond-street, and saw the prisoner carrying a basket. I stopped him, and asked what he had got. After some hesitation, he said a glass domb; that he was going to do a job for himself with it; on our way to the office, he said he would tell me the truth; that he worked for Mr. Smeethart, in Bond-street, and took the domb to force a few plants in his garden.
HORATIO TAYLOR. I cannot swear to the domb - we have so many we could not miss it.
Cross-examined by MR. ALLEY. Q. How many men have you - A. Five; he was eleven years with us, and was never allowed to take things off the premises.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18230910-309]
1279. ANN PENNY was indicted for stealing, on the 29th of August, a child's pincloth, value 15 d., the goods of Elizabeth Holroyd.
ELIZABETH HOLROYD. I live in Bluegate-fields. I lost a child's pinafore. On the 29th of August, I put it in the yard to dry, between two and three o'clock in the day. I missed it about six, and have never found it since.
ELIZA SIMPSON. I was sitting at work opposite the prosecutrix's house, and saw the prisoner take the pinafore off the line, and come out with it - she rolled it up - I heard of the robbery afterwards, and told what I knew.
Prisoner's Defence. I went into this house at five o'clock, and stopped till eight, when a person came and told me what I was charged with. I immediately went to the prosecutrix; she fell upon me and tore my bonnet off my head - I told her to send for an officer - she sent to my husband, and said, if he would give her 5 l., she would make it up, and he gave her 1 l. The officer saw her strike me.
ELIZABETH HOLROYD. I never lifted my hand against her, or spoke to her husband about anything of the kind.
STEPHEN CADMAN. I am an officer - I did not see her strike her, or ever hear that she had.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18230910-310]
1280. THOMAS WICKS was indicted for stealing, on the 17th of August, a watch, value 30 s., the goods of Thomas Rigby, from his person.
THOMAS RIGBY. I live in the New-road, St. George's. On the 17th of August, about half-past nine o'clock, I was in Whitechapel-road; I had a child in my arms, and my housekeeper by my side. I passed a doctor's shop, at the corner of North-street - the prisoner gave me a shove and turned me right round - there was a gas-light at the corner, and at the doctor's shop also - he came from behind, and shoved me, turned me right round, and snatched my watch from my fob, and ran up North-street - I pursued, calling out Stop thief! he was not taken then - I saw him in custody four days after, and cannot swear to him, as I only saw his back.
Cross-examined by MR. ALLEY. Q. Did you recover your watch - A. Yes; it was sent to me a week or ten days after.
SARAH RIGBY. I was in company with Mrs. Rigby, walking on his left side - he was carrying the child. I noticed a man following us, and looked round several times, thinking he knew us - he was alone. At the corner of North-street, he came behind us and gave Mr. Rigby a shove on the left shoulder, I did not see him snatch the watch - he ran down North-street; an alarm was given. Mr. Rigby ran after him - I took the child from him, and another person came behind and threw him down - I saw the prisoner in custody on the 22d - there was a gas light from the shop, and one from the corner - I believe him to be the man, and am sure that he is.
Cross-examined. Q. You never knew him before - A. No; I never fixed on any other - I was coming out of a friend's house in North-street, and Mr. Rigby said,
"This looks like the man who tripped me up." I said,
"It is not him, for he was a great deal taller." I described him to the officers, and when I saw the prisoner, I was certain of him.
GEORGE ELLIS. I am an officer. Mr. and Mrs. Rigby gave me a description of the men on the Monday after the robbery, and in consequence of the woman's description, I apprehended the prisoner on the Wednesday following. I knew him before. The woman never hesitated about him.
GUILTY. Aged 20.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18230910-311]
1281. PATRICK COLLINS was indicted for stealing, on the 27th of June, a waistcoat, value 6 s., the goods of William Howes.
WILLIAM HOWES. I am a pawnbroker, and live in High-street, St. Giles's. On the 27th of June I lost a waistcoat from my door post, just about eight o'clock in the morning.
CHARLES PERRY. I am a pawnbroker, and live in Museum-street, about twenty minutes past nine o'clock, the prisoner pawned this waistcoat for 2 s. - he came again about one o'clock, for another shilling on it, and I detained him. I am sure he pawned it.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Did you know him before - A. No.
WILLIAM HOWES. I have no mark on them, but know them to be mine.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18230910-312]
1282. ABBY SULLIVAN and ELLEN DONOVAN were indicted for stealing, on the 11th of September, five yards of printed cotton, value 10 s., the goods of Edward Price.
SARAH PRICE. I am a fishmonger, and live in Brill-row, Somers-town. On the 11th of September, between four and five o'clock in the afternoon, the prisoners came in and had three pennyworth of oysters. Sullivan came first, and said, wait until my sister comes; and then Donovan came in, and they eat the oysters between them, and left the shop together. They returned in a quarter of an hour, and when they were gone I missed a gown-piece of five yards and a quarter of printed cotton out of a band-box on the counter. I had not left the shop. They were behind me, and so was the cotton. They examined the gown whilst they were in the shop, and said they liked it very much. I pursued them directly. I had put it in the box again. Nobody but them were there till I missed it.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Another woman was in the shop - A. No; one came in after they were gone, and asked to see it. I looked, and it was gone. I had drank three glasses of gin and part of a pot of beer, but knew what I was about, and drank with the prisoners in the shop. When they were taken, they acknowledged to taking it and pawning it. They said for God's sake be merciful to us - we will never do such a thing again - we did it out of a lark, and said they had torn the duplicate up.
W - B - TOMLINSON. I am a pawnbroker in of Lower Wilmot-street, Brunswick-square. On the 11th of September, between five and six o'clock in the evening, the prisoners came and pawned this cotton. They said they gave 2 s. 6 d. for it. Price was very much in liquor.
Cross-examined. Q. I believe many people buy goods at tally-shops and pawn them - A. Yes.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
WILLIAM CLARK. I am a beadle. The prosecutrix pointed the prisoners out to me in Newton-street. I took Sullivan to the watch-house; the other followed. I locked them both up. They said they had pawned the gown, and had torn up the duplicate.
SULLIVAN'S Defence. I was passing her door. She called me in, and asked for drink. We drank together, and I lent her 1 s.
DONOVAN'S Defence. We had beer and gin together, and we had some oysters of her. She lent Sullivan the gown to pawn till the morning, when she would give her the money.
SULLIVAN - GUILTY.
DONOVAN - GUILTY.
Recommended to Mercy by the Jury. - Confined 2 Months.
